{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01AHS2bbersvgQXqdLeS7KM4","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→Similar to previous work, we designed a visual discrimination task in head-fixed mice running through linear virtual reality corridors (Fig. 1a). Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as ‘frozen’ crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as ‘leaf’ and ‘circle’, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (‘rock’ and ‘bricks’; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli ‘leaf2’ and ‘circle2’, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (‘leaf3’) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\n     2→\n     3→Mice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\n     4→\n     5→Before and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\n     6→\n     7→It is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \n     8→\n     9→Methods\n    10→\n    11→We performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s × camK2a-tTa mice.\n    12→\n    13→The mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\n    14→\n    15→Visual stimuli\n    16→\n    17→We showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270° of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4 m long, with 2 m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6 cm s−1, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60 cm s−1) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\n    18→\n    19→The virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0° and 45°) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (‘unsupervised gratings’ and VRg mice).\n    20→\n    21→For behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\n    22→\n    23→For imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\n    24→\n    25→The two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\n    26→\n    27→Behavioural training\n    28→\n    29→All animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5–1 h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1 h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality + reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\n    30→\n    31→For the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality + reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2 m and 3 m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2–3 m.\n    32→\n    33→For imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5 m and 3.5 m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5 m and 3.5 m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\n    34→\n    35→Processing of calcium imaging data\n    36→\n    37→Calcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Suite2p performs motion correction, region of interest detection, cell classification, neuropil correction and spike deconvolution as previously described64. For non-negative deconvolution, we used a timescale of decay of 0.75 s (ref. 65). All our analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces.\n    38→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01KofpUqdLHjtnGxSME9v8vW","session_id":"0a2bea60-a752-49c7-952f-0716c4e26b2e","uuid":"9b41ad57-5c0c-4ae5-9a02-de8c7dd18b6b","timestamp":"2026-03-23T23:51:00.960Z"}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_016TjUwc4tu6FzqpZbvtEztQ","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→Similar to previous work, we designed a visual discrimination task in head-fixed mice running through linear virtual reality corridors (Fig. 1a). Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as ‘frozen’ crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as ‘leaf’ and ‘circle’, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (‘rock’ and ‘bricks’; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli ‘leaf2’ and ‘circle2’, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (‘leaf3’) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\n     2→\n     3→Mice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\n     4→\n     5→Before and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\n     6→\n     7→It is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \n     8→\n     9→Methods\n    10→\n    11→We performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s × camK2a-tTa mice.\n    12→\n    13→The mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\n    14→\n    15→Visual stimuli\n    16→\n    17→We showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270° of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4 m long, with 2 m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6 cm s−1, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60 cm s−1) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\n    18→\n    19→The virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0° and 45°) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (‘unsupervised gratings’ and VRg mice).\n    20→\n    21→For behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\n    22→\n    23→For imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\n    24→\n    25→The two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\n    26→\n    27→Behavioural training\n    28→\n    29→All animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5–1 h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1 h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality + reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\n    30→\n    31→For the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality + reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2 m and 3 m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2–3 m.\n    32→\n    33→For imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5 m and 3.5 m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5 m and 3.5 m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\n    34→\n    35→Processing of calcium imaging data\n    36→\n    37→Calcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Suite2p performs motion correction, region of interest detection, cell classification, neuropil correction and spike deconvolution as previously described64. For non-negative deconvolution, we used a timescale of decay of 0.75 s (ref. 65). All our analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces.\n    38→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01KofpUqdLHjtnGxSME9v8vW","session_id":"0a2bea60-a752-49c7-952f-0716c4e26b2e","uuid":"b1a5f403-9f25-42bb-9c22-61417f4b15b5","timestamp":"2026-03-23T23:51:03.604Z"}

first lick frame index\n   925→            lickFr_offset = lickFr - F_id # align lick frame to first lick\n   926→            temp_spk = spk[F_id - ranges[0] : F_id+ranges[1]]\n   927→            if len(temp_spk)>0:\n   928→                Flick_pos[n] = lickPos[idx][0]\n   929→                spk2Flick[n] = spk[F_id - ranges[0] : F_id+ranges[1]]\n   930→                temp_lick = lickFr_offset[(lickFr_offset>=-ranges[0]) & (lickFr_offset<=ranges[1])] + ranges[0]\n   931→                hist, edge = np.histogram(temp_lick, bins=bins, range=(0, np.sum(ranges)))\n   932→                lick2Flick[n] = hist\n   933→    return Flick_pos, spk2Flick, lick2Flick\n   934→\n   935→def spk_2_cue(spk, beh, ranges=[15, 15], bins=30):\n   936→    \"\"\"ranges: frames included before and after cue frame\n   937→        spk: one dimensional activity\n   938→    \"\"\"\n   939→    SoundFr, lickFr, lickTr, ntrials  = beh['SoundFr'].astype(int), beh['LickFr'].astype(int), beh['LickTrind'].astype(int), beh['ntrials']\n   940→    spk2Cue = np.empty((ntrials, np.sum(ranges))) * np.nan\n   941→    lick2Cue = np.empty((ntrials, np.sum(ranges))) * np.nan\n   942→    for n in range(ntrials):\n   943→        spk2Cue[n] = spk[SoundFr[n] - ranges[0] : SoundFr[n] + ranges[1]]\n   944→        \n   945→        temp_lick = lickFr[(lickFr>=SoundFr[n]-ranges[0]) & (lickFr<=SoundFr[n]+ranges[1])] - SoundFr[n] + ranges[0]\n   946→        hist, edge = np.histogram(temp_lick, bins=bins, range=(0, np.sum(ranges)))"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":"toolu_01KofpUqdLHjtnGxSME9v8vW","session_id":"0a2bea60-a752-49c7-952f-0716c4e26b2e","uuid":"b5542b19-15cb-49da-b7c7-bbe8cdda25ad","timestamp":"2026-03-23T23:51:10.439Z"}

{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_011e98Xq33fJYSAhbRWcFZKJ","type":"tool_result","content":"     1→Similar to previous work, we designed a visual discrimination task in head-fixed mice running through linear virtual reality corridors (Fig. 1a). Mice had to discriminate between visual texture patterns in two corridors; these corridors were repeated in pseudo-random order. The visual patterns in each corridor were obtained as ‘frozen’ crops from large photographs of naturalistic textures. For simplicity, we denote the stimuli as ‘leaf’ and ‘circle’, even though other visual stimuli were also used in some mice (‘rock’ and ‘bricks’; see Methods and Extended Data Fig. 1a). The visual stimuli were spatial frequency matched between categories to encourage the use of higher-order visual features in discrimination. A sound cue was presented at a random position inside each corridor and was followed by the availability of water in rewarded trials only (Fig. 1a). After approximately 2 weeks of training (Fig. 1b), mice demonstrated selective licking in the rewarded corridor in anticipation of reward delivery (Fig. 1c,d; error bars on all figures represent s.e.m.). After learning, we introduced unrewarded test stimuli ‘leaf2’ and ‘circle2’, which were different frozen crops of the same photographs. We then continued training with unrewarded leaf2 until the mice stopped licking to this stimulus, at which point we introduced another test stimulus (‘leaf3’) as well as spatially shuffled versions of leaf1 (Fig. 1b).\n     2→\n     3→Mice in the unsupervised cohort also ran through the same corridors for similar periods of time, but did not receive water rewards and were not water restricted. We also studied a cohort of mice that ran through a virtual reality corridor with gratings on the walls. These served as a control to show the effect of simple exposure to virtual reality, independent of the image patterns shown. To ensure that all mice had a comparable visual experience, we fixed the speed of the virtual reality when mice ran faster than a speed threshold, and kept the virtual reality stationary otherwise. The overall running speeds were similar before and after learning, and between the task and unsupervised cohorts (Extended Data Fig. 2). We only considered timepoints during running for analysis, which removed time periods when the task mice stopped to collect water rewards.\n     4→\n     5→Before and after learning, we recorded from large neural populations across many visual areas simultaneously using a two-photon mesoscope (Fig. 1e). We ran Suite2p on this data to obtain the activity traces from 20,547 to 89,577 neurons in each recording.\n     6→\n     7→It is possible that the neural plasticity that we observed was due to spatial learning and navigation signals, which have been found to modulate firing rates even in the visual cortex. Alternatively, the neural plasticity might be due to adaptation to the visual statistics of the natural images that we presented. To distinguish between a spatial and a visual plasticity hypothesis, we next introduced two unrewarded test stimuli, leaf2 and circle2, which contained similar visual features to leaf1 and circle1, respectively, but were arranged in different spatial configurations (Fig. 2a). \n     8→\n     9→Methods\n    10→\n    11→We performed 89 recordings in 19 mice bred to express GCaMP6s in excitatory neurons: TetO-GCaMP6s × camK2a-tTa mice.\n    12→\n    13→The mice were free to run on an air-floating ball.\n    14→\n    15→Visual stimuli\n    16→\n    17→We showed virtual reality corridors to the mice on three perpendicular LED tablet screens, which surrounded each mouse (covering 270° of their visual field of view). To present the stimuli, we used PsychToolbox-3 in MATLAB. The virtual reality corridors were each 4 m long, with 2 m of grey space between corridors. The corridors were shown in a random order. The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6 cm s−1, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60 cm s−1) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\n    18→\n    19→The virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0° and 45°) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (‘unsupervised gratings’ and VRg mice).\n    20→\n    21→For behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\n    22→\n    23→For imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\n    24→\n    25→The two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\n    26→\n    27→Behavioural training\n    28→\n    29→All animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5–1 h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1 h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality + reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\n    30→\n    31→For the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality + reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2 m and 3 m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2–3 m.\n    32→\n    33→For imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5 m and 3.5 m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5 m and 3.5 m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\n    34→\n    35→Processing of calcium imaging data\n    36→\n    37→Calcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Suite2p performs motion correction, region of interest detection, cell classification, neuropil correction and spike deconvolution as previously described64. For non-negative deconvolution, we used a timescale of decay of 0.75 s (ref. 65). All our analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces.\n    38→"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"0a2bea60-a752-49c7-952f-0716c4e26b2e","uuid":"3edaca19-e540-4978-8c9b-e5f70f3502e4","timestamp":"2026-03-23T23:51:46.951Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/app/methods.txt","content":"Similar to previous work, we designed a visual discrimination task in head-fixed mice running through linear virtual reality corridors (Fig. 1a). The mice moved forward in the virtual reality corridors by running faster than a threshold of 6 cm s−1, but the virtual corridors always moved at a constant speed (60 cm s−1) as long as mice kept running faster than the threshold. Running was detected using an optical tracking sensor placed close to the ball.\n\nThe virtual reality corridors were created by concatenating four random crops from one of four large texture images: circle, leaf, rock and brick (Extended Data Fig. 1a). Grating stimuli (with angles of 0° and 45°) were also used to create the virtual reality corridors for a subset of mice (‘unsupervised gratings’ and VRg mice).\n\nFor behaviour-only experiments, each mouse was trained with rewards on one random pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. Mice with unsupervised pretraining (VRn mice) were pretrained on the same pair on which they were trained with rewards.\n\nFor imaging experiments, mice from both the task and the unsupervised cohorts were only trained on or exposed to one pair of stimuli, such as leaf–circle or rock–brick. For the mice exposed to the grating stimuli, we presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli before and after exposure to the gratings stimuli and recorded the neural responses to the naturalistic stimuli pairs, so each mouse can have more than one imaging session before and after. We also presented more than one pair of naturalistic stimuli to the naive mice, so each naive mouse can have more than one imaging session for testing naive responses.\n\nThe two different types of leaf1-swap stimuli (Extended Data Figs. 6f and 7a) were introduced separately for task mice but are pooled together for statistics analysis. The two leaf1-swap stimuli were introduced in the same session for unsupervised and naive mice, but were treated as two different data points and pooled together for statistical analyses.\n\nBehavioural training\n\nAll animals were handled via refined handling techniques for at least 3 days before being acclimated to head fixation on the ball. Animals were acclimated gradually (0.5–1 h per day) on the ball over at least 3 days until they could be head fixed without exhibiting any signs of distress. Then, animals began a running training regiment (1 h per day), which lasted for at least 5 days to ensure they could run smoothly and continuously on the ball before being exposed to the closed-loop virtual linear corridor. For water-restricted mice, we trained them for 2 days to get used to acquiring water from the spout when no stimulus was presented, before the virtual reality + reward training session. The VRn and VRg pretraining groups of mice (Fig. 5) were trained to acquire water from the spout on the last 2 days of unsupervised pretraining with no stimuli presented, after the virtual reality session ended to avoid the associative learning between stimuli and rewards. For the group without pretraining (Fig. 5), learning to get reward from the spout was similarly carried out after the running training session on the last 2 days of running training.\n\nFor the behaviour-only experiment, all animals started training in the virtual reality + reward training session on a Monday and continued training for exactly 5 days. The first day of training consisted of passive reward training, during which the reward was always delivered to the mouse in the rewarded corridor at the beginning of the reward zone. The next 4 days consisted of active reward training, during which the mice were required to lick in the reward zone to trigger the reward. This ensured a consistent training schedule during the critical learning period. The beginning of the reward zone was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between 2 m and 3 m, and continued until the end of the corridor. This meant that the earliest position in the rewarded corridor in which the mouse can receive the reward was a range of 2–3 m.\n\nFor imaging mice, the sound cue was presented in all trial types, for task and unsupervised mice, and the time of the sound cue was randomly chosen per trial from a uniform distribution between positions 0.5 m and 3.5 m. For task mice, the sound cue indicated the beginning of the reward zone in the rewarded corridor. The reward was delivered if a lick was detected after the sound cue in the rewarded corridor. As mice kept licking (anticipatory licking) as soon as they entered the reward corridors and learned to lick right after the sound cue, the reward delivery locations were also approximately uniformly distributed between 0.5 m and 3.5 m (Fig. 1c). In some mice, the reward was delivered passively with a delay after the sound cue, but these mice still showed anticipatory licking before the sound cue. To rule out whether the licking was due to detecting signals related to the reward delivery, such as water coming out the spout or sound produced by solenoid valve, we considered a lick response if the mouse licked at least once inside the corridor but before the sound cue. Although the rewards were absent, the sound cue was still presented in the unsupervised training experiment for consistency.\n\nProcessing of calcium imaging data\n\nCalcium imaging data were processed using Suite2p32, available on GitHub (www.github.com/MouseLand/suite2p). Suite2p performs motion correction, region of interest detection, cell classification, neuropil correction and spike deconvolution as previously described64. For non-negative deconvolution, we used a timescale of decay of 0.75 s (ref. 65). All our analyses were based on deconvolved fluorescence traces.\n","numLines":38,"startLine":1,"totalLines":38}}}

dprime(x1, x2):\n    \"\"\"x1,x2: neurons * frames \"\"\"\n    u1, u2 = np.nanmean(x1, 1), np.nanmean(x2, 1)\n    sig1, sig2 = np.nanstd(x1, 1), np.nanstd(x2, 1)\n    return 2 * (u1 - u2) / (sig1 + sig2)      \n\ndef get_dist_img(xpos, ypos, sel_idx=None, sig=30, sig_bg=30, xoff=800, yoff=800):\n    from scipy import ndimage\n    \"\"\"create an binary image for indicate neurons' position using xpos and ypos\n    \"\"\"\n    x, y = xpos+xoff, ypos+yoff\n    bg, img = np.zeros((5000,5000)), np.zeros((5000,5000))\n    bg[y.astype(int), x.astype(int)] = 1.0\n    bg = ndimage.gaussian_filter(bg, sigma=1, truncate=sig_bg)>0 # create a background map\n    bg = bg.astype(float)\n    bg[bg==False] = np.nan # fill empty space with nan\n    \n    if any(sel_idx != None):\n        img[y[sel_idx].astype(int), x[sel_idx].astype(int)] = 1\n    img = ndimage.gaussian_filter(img, sigma=sig)\n    img[np.isnan(bg)] = np.nan\n    img = np.fliplr(img)\n    return img    \n\ndef How are **all the data** for all subjects, sessions, and trials loaded in?\n\ni. The AI iterates over all `Beh_*.npy` files in `data/beh/`, extracting session records keyed by `<mouse>_<date>_<blk>` identifiers. It deduplicates to 89 unique recording bases (matching the paper's \"89 recordings in 19 mice\"). For each session, it loads neural data from `data/spk/<base>_neural_data.npy` and retinotopy from `data/retinotopy/<mouse>_<date>_trans.npz`. Behavior-only pretraining data in `Unsupervised_pretraining_behavior/` is excluded since there are no matching neural recordings. Experiment metadata from `Imaging_Exp_info.npy` is used to annotate experiment types.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef select_representative_sessions(root: Path) -> list[SessionSpec]:\n    beh_dir = root / \"data\" / \"beh\"\n    base_to_experiments = load_experiment_type_map(root)\n    selected: dict[str, SessionSpec] = {}\n    for beh_path in sorted(beh_dir.glob(\"Beh_*.npy\")):\n        exp_type = beh_path.stem.replace(\"Beh_\", \"\")\n        beh_dict = np.load(beh_path, allow_pickle=True).item()\n        for key, record in beh_dict.items():\n            base = \"_\".join(key.split(\"_\")[:5])\n            ...\n```\n\n```python\ndef load_spike_matrix(root: Path, base: str) -> np.ndarray:\n    path = root / \"data\" / \"spk\" / f\"{base}_neural_data.npy\"\n    spk_item = np.load(path, allow_pickle=True).item()\n    spk = np.concatenate([plane for plane in spk_item[\"spks\"]], axis=0)\n    return spk.astype(np.float32, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. The AI documented in CONVERSION_NOTES.md that there are 142 experiment-session records across 23 experiment types, collapsing to 99 unique behavior session keys and 89 unique neural recordings. It chose to use 89 unique recording bases as sessions to match the paper's count and avoid data duplication.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects (mice)?\n\ni. Subject names are parsed from the first component of the recording base string (e.g., `TX108` from `TX108_2023_03_25_1`). Unique subjects are collected and sorted alphabetically to create a deterministic `subjects` list and `subject_idx` mapping.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef parse_base(base: str) -> tuple[str, datetime, str]:\n    parts = base.split(\"_\")\n    subject = parts[0]\n    date = datetime.strptime(\"_\".join(parts[1:4]), \"%Y_%m_%d\")\n    blk = parts[4]\n    return subject, date, blk\n```\n\n```python\nsubject_names = sorted({spec.subject for spec in specs})\nsubject_to_idx = {name: idx for idx, name in enumerate(subject_names)}\n```\n\niii. The AI noted that 19 unique imaging mice are expected from the paper and data, and confirmed this count in its validation. The approach matches how the reference code identifies mice via `db['mname']`.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each unique recording base `<mouse>_<date>_<blk>` is treated as one session. When multiple behavior keys share the same recording base (e.g., plain key and swap variants), a scoring function selects the best representative key, preferring keys without \"swap\" and with more valid stimulus IDs.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef session_key_score(key: str, record: dict) -> tuple[int, int, int, str]:\n    stim_id = np.asarray(record.get(\"stim_id\", []), dtype=float)\n    non_nan_stim = int(np.isfinite(stim_id).sum()) if stim_id.size else 0\n    unique_walls = int(len(np.unique(record.get(\"WallName\", []))))\n    swap_penalty = 1 if \"swap\" in key else 0\n    return (swap_penalty, -non_nan_stim, -unique_walls, key)\n```\n\niii. The AI justified this by noting that different experiment labels can reference the same neural recording, and that choosing one representative behavior key per base avoids data leakage. This yields 89 sessions matching the paper.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are identified using the frame-level `ft_trInd` array, which assigns each imaging frame to a trial index. For each of the `ntrials` trials in a session, frame indices belonging to that trial are collected, filtered to running corridor frames.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef build_trial_frame_indices(record: dict, nfr: int) -> list[np.ndarray]:\n    ft_tr = np.asarray(record[\"ft_trInd\"][:nfr], dtype=float)\n    valid = np.isfinite(ft_tr)\n    valid_idx = np.flatnonzero(valid)\n    trial_ids = ft_tr[valid].astype(int)\n    keep = np.asarray(record[\"ft_CorrSpc\"][:nfr], dtype=bool)[valid]\n    keep &= np.asarray(record[\"ft_move\"][:nfr], dtype=float)[valid] > 0\n    ntrials = int(record[\"ntrials\"])\n    frame_indices: list[np.ndarray] = []\n    for trial in range(ntrials):\n        trial_frames = valid_idx[keep & (trial_ids == trial)]\n        frame_indices.append(trial_frames.astype(np.int32, copy=False))\n    return frame_indices\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that it uses `ft_trInd` for trial assignment rather than `StartFr`/`EndFr`, matching the reference code convention. Only running corridor frames (`ft_CorrSpc & ft_move > 0`) are retained per trial.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials are filtered by: (1) must have at least 5 retained timepoints (`MIN_TRIAL_TIMEPOINTS = 5`), (2) retained duration since trial start must be < 60 seconds, (3) maximum interframe gap must be < 10 seconds. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials after filtering are excluded entirely.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_passes_quality_filters(record, trial_idx, frame_idx, nfr):\n    frame_idx = np.asarray(frame_idx, dtype=np.int64)\n    frame_idx = frame_idx[frame_idx < nfr]\n    if frame_idx.size < MIN_TRIAL_TIMEPOINTS:\n        return False, \"too_few_timepoints\"\n    frame_times = np.asarray(record[\"ft\"][:nfr], dtype=float)[frame_idx]\n    retained_duration_s = float((frame_times[-1] - float(record[\"Trial_start_time\"][trial_idx])) * SEC_PER_DAY)\n    if retained_duration_s > MAX_RETAINED_TRIAL_DURATION_S:\n        return False, \"retained_duration_gt_60s\"\n    if frame_idx.size > 1:\n        max_gap_s = float(np.max(np.diff(frame_times)) * SEC_PER_DAY)\n        if max_gap_s > MAX_RETAINED_INTERFRAME_GAP_S:\n            return False, \"interframe_gap_gt_10s\"\n    return True, None\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that 2,217 trials were removed by the duration and gap filters (reducing from 38,110 to 35,893 trials). These were described as \"decoder-pathological stalled trials\" with sparse late running segments. The corridor + running filters match the reference code; the duration and gap filters are additional quality controls not present in the reference.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from the `spks` arrays stored in `<base>_neural_data.npy` files. Each file contains a dict with key `spks`, which is a list of arrays (one per imaging plane) that are concatenated along the neuron dimension.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_spike_matrix(root: Path, base: str) -> np.ndarray:\n    path = root / \"data\" / \"spk\" / f\"{base}_neural_data.npy\"\n    spk_item = np.load(path, allow_pickle=True).item()\n    spk = np.concatenate([plane for plane in spk_item[\"spks\"]], axis=0)\n    return spk.astype(np.float32, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. The AI confirmed in CONVERSION_NOTES.md that these are deconvolved fluorescence traces from Suite2p processing and that no delta-F/F computation is needed, consistent with the paper and reference code.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. After loading and concatenating planes, the AI applies a neuron selection step: it computes stimulus d-prime on running corridor frames, identifies corridor-responsive neurons (activity > gray space activity), then selects the top 5% positive and 5% negative d' neurons per visual area (V1, mHV, lHV, aHV). This reduces the neuron count from ~50K to ~3.4K per session on average.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef select_decoder_neurons(spk, record, region_idx):\n    nfr = spk.shape[1]\n    stim_pair = choose_reference_stimuli(record)\n    if stim_pair is None:\n        return np.arange(spk.shape[0], dtype=np.int32)\n    ...\n    dp = dprime(spk[:, stim1_mask], spk[:, stim2_mask])\n    corr_neu = (\n        (np.mean(spk[:, stim1_mask], axis=1) > np.mean(spk[:, gray_mask], axis=1))\n        | (np.mean(spk[:, stim2_mask], axis=1) > np.mean(spk[:, gray_mask], axis=1))\n    )\n    selected = np.zeros(spk.shape[0], dtype=bool)\n    for area in range(4):\n        candidates = corr_neu & (region_idx == area) & np.isfinite(dp)\n        ...\n        hi, lo = np.nanpercentile(dp[candidates], [95, 5])\n        selected |= candidates & ((dp >= hi) | (dp <= lo))\n    return np.flatnonzero(selected).astype(np.int32)\n```\n\niii. The AI justified this as following the reference coding-direction analysis (`Get_coding_direction`, `Get_sort_spk`) which uses top 5% positive/5% negative selective neurons per area. However, this selection is analysis-specific in the reference code and is not a general neuron filtering step.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two quality filters are applied: (1) only corridor-responsive neurons (mean activity in corridor > mean activity in gray space), and (2) the top 5%/bottom 5% d' selection per visual area. Additionally, neurons not assignable to V1/mHV/lHV/aHV are excluded.\n\nii. (Same `select_decoder_neurons` code as 2-b above)\n\niii. The AI noted in CONVERSION_NOTES.md that Suite2p already classifies cells and the reference code has no additional global neuron quality filter, but applied the analysis-specific selectivity filter for \"tractable full-dataset decoding.\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to trial start (corridor entry) by selecting frames belonging to each trial via `ft_trInd`. Only running frames inside the textured corridor (`ft_CorrSpc & ft_move > 0`) are retained. The first retained frame of each trial is near the corridor entry point.\n\nii.\n```python\nneural_trial = spk[:, frame_idx].astype(np.float32, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. It chose to use 89 unique recording bases as sessions to match the paper's count and avoid data duplication.\n\n## 1-b. How are the data split into subjects (mice)?\n\ni. Subject names are parsed from the first component of the recording base string (e.g., `TX108` from `TX108_2023_03_25_1`). Unique subjects are collected and sorted alphabetically to create a deterministic `subjects` list and `subject_idx` mapping.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef parse_base(base: str) -> tuple[str, datetime, str]:\n    parts = base.split(\"_\")\n    subject = parts[0]\n    date = datetime.strptime(\"_\".join(parts[1:4]), \"%Y_%m_%d\")\n    blk = parts[4]\n    return subject, date, blk\n```\n\n```python\nsubject_names = sorted({spec.subject for spec in specs})\nsubject_to_idx = {name: idx for idx, name in enumerate(subject_names)}\n```\n\niii. The AI noted that 19 unique imaging mice are expected from the paper and data, and confirmed this count in its validation. The approach matches how the reference code identifies mice via `db['mname']`.\n\n## 1-c. How are the data split into sessions?\n\ni. Each unique recording base `<mouse>_<date>_<blk>` is treated as one session. When multiple behavior keys share the same recording base (e.g., plain key and swap variants), a scoring function selects the best representative key, preferring keys without \"swap\" and with more valid stimulus IDs.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef session_key_score(key: str, record: dict) -> tuple[int, int, int, str]:\n    stim_id = np.asarray(record.get(\"stim_id\", []), dtype=float)\n    non_nan_stim = int(np.isfinite(stim_id).sum()) if stim_id.size else 0\n    unique_walls = int(len(np.unique(record.get(\"WallName\", []))))\n    swap_penalty = 1 if \"swap\" in key else 0\n    return (swap_penalty, -non_nan_stim, -unique_walls, key)\n```\n\niii. The AI justified this by noting that different experiment labels can reference the same neural recording, and that choosing one representative behavior key per base avoids data leakage. This yields 89 sessions matching the paper.\n\n## 1-d. How are the data split into trials?\n\ni. Trials are identified using the frame-level `ft_trInd` array, which assigns each imaging frame to a trial index. For each of the `ntrials` trials in a session, frame indices belonging to that trial are collected, filtered to running corridor frames.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef build_trial_frame_indices(record: dict, nfr: int) -> list[np.ndarray]:\n    ft_tr = np.asarray(record[\"ft_trInd\"][:nfr], dtype=float)\n    valid = np.isfinite(ft_tr)\n    valid_idx = np.flatnonzero(valid)\n    trial_ids = ft_tr[valid].astype(int)\n    keep = np.asarray(record[\"ft_CorrSpc\"][:nfr], dtype=bool)[valid]\n    keep &= np.asarray(record[\"ft_move\"][:nfr], dtype=float)[valid] > 0\n    ntrials = int(record[\"ntrials\"])\n    frame_indices: list[np.ndarray] = []\n    for trial in range(ntrials):\n        trial_frames = valid_idx[keep & (trial_ids == trial)]\n        frame_indices.append(trial_frames.astype(np.int32, copy=False))\n    return frame_indices\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that it uses `ft_trInd` for trial assignment rather than `StartFr`/`EndFr`, matching the reference code convention. Only running corridor frames (`ft_CorrSpc & ft_move > 0`) are retained per trial.\n\n## 1-e. How are trials filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Trials are filtered by: (1) must have at least 5 retained timepoints (`MIN_TRIAL_TIMEPOINTS = 5`), (2) retained duration since trial start must be < 60 seconds, (3) maximum interframe gap must be < 10 seconds. Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials after filtering are excluded entirely.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef trial_passes_quality_filters(record, trial_idx, frame_idx, nfr):\n    frame_idx = np.asarray(frame_idx, dtype=np.int64)\n    frame_idx = frame_idx[frame_idx < nfr]\n    if frame_idx.size < MIN_TRIAL_TIMEPOINTS:\n        return False, \"too_few_timepoints\"\n    frame_times = np.asarray(record[\"ft\"][:nfr], dtype=float)[frame_idx]\n    retained_duration_s = float((frame_times[-1] - float(record[\"Trial_start_time\"][trial_idx])) * SEC_PER_DAY)\n    if retained_duration_s > MAX_RETAINED_TRIAL_DURATION_S:\n        return False, \"retained_duration_gt_60s\"\n    if frame_idx.size > 1:\n        max_gap_s = float(np.max(np.diff(frame_times)) * SEC_PER_DAY)\n        if max_gap_s > MAX_RETAINED_INTERFRAME_GAP_S:\n            return False, \"interframe_gap_gt_10s\"\n    return True, None\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that 2,217 trials were removed by the duration and gap filters (reducing from 38,110 to 35,893 trials). These were described as \"decoder-pathological stalled trials\" with sparse late running segments. The corridor + running filters match the reference code; the duration and gap filters are additional quality controls not present in the reference.\n\n## 2-a. What variables in the raw data is the `neural` data derived from?\n\ni. Neural data is derived from the `spks` arrays stored in `<base>_neural_data.npy` files. Each file contains a dict with key `spks`, which is a list of arrays (one per imaging plane) that are concatenated along the neuron dimension.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef load_spike_matrix(root: Path, base: str) -> np.ndarray:\n    path = root / \"data\" / \"spk\" / f\"{base}_neural_data.npy\"\n    spk_item = np.load(path, allow_pickle=True).item()\n    spk = np.concatenate([plane for plane in spk_item[\"spks\"]], axis=0)\n    return spk.astype(np.float32, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. The AI confirmed in CONVERSION_NOTES.md that these are deconvolved fluorescence traces from Suite2p processing and that no delta-F/F computation is needed, consistent with the paper and reference code.\n\n## 2-b. How is the `neural` data processed?\n\ni. After loading and concatenating planes, the AI applies a neuron selection step: it computes stimulus d-prime on running corridor frames, identifies corridor-responsive neurons (activity > gray space activity), then selects the top 5% positive and 5% negative d' neurons per visual area (V1, mHV, lHV, aHV). This reduces the neuron count from ~50K to ~3.4K per session on average.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef select_decoder_neurons(spk, record, region_idx):\n    nfr = spk.shape[1]\n    stim_pair = choose_reference_stimuli(record)\n    if stim_pair is None:\n        return np.arange(spk.shape[0], dtype=np.int32)\n    ...\n    dp = dprime(spk[:, stim1_mask], spk[:, stim2_mask])\n    corr_neu = (\n        (np.mean(spk[:, stim1_mask], axis=1) > np.mean(spk[:, gray_mask], axis=1))\n        | (np.mean(spk[:, stim2_mask], axis=1) > np.mean(spk[:, gray_mask], axis=1))\n    )\n    selected = np.zeros(spk.shape[0], dtype=bool)\n    for area in range(4):\n        candidates = corr_neu & (region_idx == area) & np.isfinite(dp)\n        ...\n        hi, lo = np.nanpercentile(dp[candidates], [95, 5])\n        selected |= candidates & ((dp >= hi) | (dp <= lo))\n    return np.flatnonzero(selected).astype(np.int32)\n```\n\niii. The AI justified this as following the reference coding-direction analysis (`Get_coding_direction`, `Get_sort_spk`) which uses top 5% positive/5% negative selective neurons per area. However, this selection is analysis-specific in the reference code and is not a general neuron filtering step.\n\n## 2-c. How is the `neural` data filtered based on quality controls?\n\ni. Two quality filters are applied: (1) only corridor-responsive neurons (mean activity in corridor > mean activity in gray space), and (2) the top 5%/bottom 5% d' selection per visual area. Additionally, neurons not assignable to V1/mHV/lHV/aHV are excluded.\n\nii. (Same `select_decoder_neurons` code as 2-b above)\n\niii. The AI noted in CONVERSION_NOTES.md that Suite2p already classifies cells and the reference code has no additional global neuron quality filter, but applied the analysis-specific selectivity filter for \"tractable full-dataset decoding.\"\n\n## 2-d. How is the per-trial `neural` data aligned to the event described in the `instructions`?\n\ni. Neural data is aligned to trial start (corridor entry) by selecting frames belonging to each trial via `ft_trInd`. Only running frames inside the textured corridor (`ft_CorrSpc & ft_move > 0`) are retained. The first retained frame of each trial is near the corridor entry point.\n\nii.\n```python\nneural_trial = spk[:, frame_idx].astype(np.float32, copy=False)\n```\n\niii. The AI set `temporal_alignment_event` to \"corridor entry / trial start\" and `off_start` to 0.0, indicating alignment starts at trial onset.\n\n## 2-e. What is the temporal resolution (time bin size) of the converted data? Is any temporal rebinning applied?\n\ni. The AI uses the native imaging frame rate (~314.7 ms median) without any temporal rebinning. The `time_bin_size` metadata field is set to the median frame interval across sessions.\n\nii.\n```python\nmetadata = {\n    ...\n    \"time_bin_size\": float(np.median(frame_dt_medians)),\n    ...\n    \"frame_bin_source\": \"native imaging frame timestamps; no temporal resampling\",\n}\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that the paper does not state a fixed ms bin size, and that keeping native frame timestamps is more faithful than interpolation to a synthetic time grid.\n\n## 3-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time to sound cue* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `SoundTime` (per-trial sound cue timestamp in MATLAB datenum format) and `ft` (per-frame timestamps in MATLAB datenum format).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef session_trial_info(record, trial, frame_idx):\n    frame_times = np.asarray(record[\"ft\"], dtype=float)[frame_idx]\n    time_to_cue = (float(record[\"SoundTime\"][trial]) - frame_times) * SEC_PER_DAY\n    ...\n    return time_to_cue.astype(np.float32), ...\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that `SoundTime` contains the cue timing for each trial and that continuous time-to-cue is appropriate for the decoder input.\n\n## 3-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time to sound cue*?\n\ni. For each retained frame, computes `(SoundTime[trial] - frame_time) * 86400` to convert from MATLAB datenum difference to seconds. Positive values indicate frames before the cue; negative values indicate frames after.\n\nii. (Same `session_trial_info` code as 3-a)\n\niii. The AI's sign convention (positive before cue, negative after) is documented in the metadata.\n\n## 3-c. How is the `input` *Time to sound cue* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Computed at exactly the same retained imaging frames as the neural data, producing one value per timepoint.\n\nii.\n```python\ntime_to_cue, time_since_start = session_trial_info(spec.record, trial_idx, frame_idx)\ninput_trial = np.vstack([time_to_cue, training_day, time_since_start, reward_available])\n```\n\niii. Both neural and input arrays index into the same `frame_idx`, ensuring perfect temporal alignment.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Day of training* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the session date parsed from the recording base name (e.g., `2023_03_25` from `TX108_2023_03_25_1`). The first session date for each mouse is tracked to compute relative days.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef parse_base(base: str) -> tuple[str, datetime, str]:\n    ...\n    date = datetime.strptime(\"_\".join(parts[1:4]), \"%Y_%m_%d\")\n    ...\n\nspecs = sorted(selected.values(), key=lambda s: (s.subject, s.date, int(s.blk)))\nfirst_date_by_subject: dict[str, datetime] = {}\nfor spec in specs:\n    first_date_by_subject.setdefault(spec.subject, spec.date)\n    spec.training_day = float((spec.date - first_date_by_subject[spec.subject]).days)\n```\n\niii. The AI noted this variable is not in the paper but is required by the decoder task. It uses elapsed days since the mouse's first retained imaging session as a reproducible continuous proxy.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Day of training*?\n\ni. Computes `(session_date - first_session_date_for_mouse).days` as a float. This per-trial scalar is broadcast to all timepoints.\n\nii.\n```python\ntraining_day = np.full(frame_idx.size, spec.training_day, dtype=np.float32)\n```\n\niii. Values range from 0.0 to 92.0 across the dataset, reflecting the span of recording dates.\n\n## 4-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Environment type* derived from?\n\ni. The AI does not have a separate \"Environment type\" input. Instead, it has `reward_available` as input[3], derived from the per-trial `isRew` array in the behavior data. This binary variable indicates whether the trial is in a rewarded corridor (1) or not (0), effectively encoding the environment type relevant to the animal's task.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_available = np.full(frame_idx.size, float(bool(spec.record[\"isRew\"][trial_idx])), dtype=np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The AI followed the decoder task specification which lists \"Reward availability: 1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not\" as the fourth decoder input, rather than a separate \"Environment type\" variable.\n\n## 4-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Environment type*?\n\ni. The `isRew` per-trial boolean is converted to float (0.0 or 1.0) and broadcast across all timepoints of the trial. No additional processing.\n\nii. (Same code as 4-a above)\n\niii. The AI documented that unsupervised and naive sessions have `isRew.sum() == 0`, meaning all trials in those sessions have `reward_available = 0`.\n\n## 5-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Time since trial start* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `ft` (per-frame timestamps) and `Trial_start_time` (per-trial start timestamps), both in MATLAB datenum format.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef session_trial_info(record, trial, frame_idx):\n    frame_times = np.asarray(record[\"ft\"], dtype=float)[frame_idx]\n    ...\n    time_since_start = (frame_times - float(record[\"Trial_start_time\"][trial])) * SEC_PER_DAY\n    return ..., time_since_start.astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The AI noted this should always be non-negative since frames come after trial start.\n\n## 5-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Time since trial start*?\n\ni. For each retained frame: `(frame_time - Trial_start_time[trial]) * 86400` to convert datenum difference to seconds. Values are always non-negative.\n\nii. (Same code as 5-a)\n\niii. Range is [0.0, 54.3] seconds across the full dataset after quality filtering.\n\n## 5-c. How is the `input` *Time since trial start* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Computed at exactly the same retained imaging frames as neural data, ensuring frame-level alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\ninput_trial = np.vstack([time_to_cue, training_day, time_since_start, reward_available])\n```\n\niii. Same frame indices used for neural, input, and output arrays.\n\n## 6-a. What variables in the raw data is `input` *Reward availability* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the per-trial `isRew` boolean array in the behavior data.\n\nii.\n```python\nreward_available = np.full(frame_idx.size, float(bool(spec.record[\"isRew\"][trial_idx])), dtype=np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that `isRew` indicates whether reward is available in that corridor on that trial. Unsupervised sessions have all zeros.\n\n## 6-b. What processing is involved in computing `input` *Reward availability*?\n\ni. The per-trial boolean `isRew[trial_idx]` is converted to float (0.0 or 1.0) and repeated for every timepoint in the trial.\n\nii. (Same code as 6-a)\n\niii. Per the decoder task specification: \"1 if in rewarded corridor, 0 if not, discrete, per-trial.\"\n\n## 7-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Visual stimulus category* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from the per-trial `WallName` array and the session-level `UniqWalls` array. A global list of all unique wall names across all retained sessions is constructed.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef collect_visual_categories(specs: list[SessionSpec]) -> list[str]:\n    categories = sorted({str(wall) for spec in specs for wall in spec.record[\"UniqWalls\"]})\n    return categories\n\nvisual_to_idx = {name: idx for idx, name in enumerate(visual_categories)}\nstim_idx = np.full(frame_idx.size, visual_to_idx[str(spec.record[\"WallName\"][trial_idx])], dtype=np.int16)\n```\n\niii. The AI found 15 unique visual stimulus categories across the imaging data. Each trial is assigned a single categorical index based on its wall name.\n\n## 7-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Visual stimulus category*?\n\ni. Each trial's `WallName` is mapped to an integer index in the global sorted list of unique wall names. This per-trial index is repeated across all timepoints. 15 categories are used.\n\nii. (Same code as 7-a)\n\niii. The AI documented the 15 categories: circle1, circle2, circle3, leaf1, leaf1_swap1, leaf1_swap2, leaf2, leaf3, rock1, rock2, wood1, wood1_swap1, wood1_swap2, wood2, wood5.\n\n## 8-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Licking* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `LickFr` (frame indices of lick events) and `LickTrind` (trial index for each lick event).\n\nii.\n```python\ndef build_lick_frame_lookup(record, nfr):\n    lick_frames = np.asarray(record[\"LickFr\"], dtype=float)\n    lick_trial = np.asarray(record[\"LickTrind\"], dtype=float)\n    valid = np.isfinite(lick_frames) & np.isfinite(lick_trial)\n    lick_frames = lick_frames[valid].astype(int)\n    lick_trial = lick_trial[valid].astype(int)\n    ...\n    lookup: dict[int, np.ndarray] = {}\n    for trial in np.unique(lick_trial):\n        lookup[int(trial)] = np.unique(lick_frames[lick_trial == trial]).astype(np.int32)\n    return lookup\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that lick events are matched to imaging frames using `LickFr` indices.\n\n## 8-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Licking*?\n\ni. For each retained frame in a trial, checks if that frame index appears in the trial's lick frame set. Produces a binary vector (0 = no lick, 1 = lick) at imaging frame resolution.\n\nii.\n```python\nlick_frames = lick_lookup.get(trial_idx, np.empty(0, dtype=np.int32))\nlicking = np.isin(frame_idx, lick_frames, assume_unique=False).astype(np.int16)\n```\n\niii. The overall lick rate across the full dataset is ~3.8% of retained frames, which is sparse as expected since retained frames are running corridor frames and mice mainly lick around the reward zone.\n\n## 8-c. How is `output` *Licking* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Lick events are identified at the same imaging frame indices used for neural data. The `np.isin(frame_idx, lick_frames)` call ensures exact frame-level alignment.\n\nii. (Same code as 8-b)\n\niii. Both neural and licking data use the same `frame_idx` array per trial.\n\n## 9-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Position in corridor* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `ft_Pos`, the per-frame position in the corridor in decimeters.\n\nii.\n```python\nposition_bin = digitize_position(np.asarray(spec.record[\"ft_Pos\"], dtype=np.float32)[frame_idx])\n```\n\niii. Raw positions are in decimeters; the corridor is 40 decimeters (4 meters) long.\n\n## 9-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Position in corridor*?\n\ni. Raw `ft_Pos` values are read at retained frame indices. No interpolation or smoothing.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef digitize_position(pos_decimeters: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n    pos = np.clip(pos_decimeters, 0.0, 39.999)\n    return np.clip((pos // 10.0).astype(np.int16), 0, 3)\n```\n\niii. Position values are clipped to [0, 39.999] decimeters before binning.\n\n## 9-c. How is `output` *Position in corridor* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Positions are divided into 4 equal 1-meter bins by integer-dividing decimeter values by 10: bin 0 = 0-1m, bin 1 = 1-2m, bin 2 = 2-3m, bin 3 = 3-4m. Clipped to valid range.\n\nii. (Same `digitize_position` code as 9-b)\n\niii. The resulting distribution is near-uniform (~25% each) across the full dataset, consistent with expected corridor traversal patterns.\n\n## 9-d. How is `output` *Position in corridor* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Position values are read at the same retained frame indices as neural data, ensuring frame-level alignment.\n\nii.\n```python\nposition_bin = digitize_position(np.asarray(spec.record[\"ft_Pos\"], dtype=np.float32)[frame_idx])\n```\n\niii. Same `frame_idx` used for neural, input, and output.\n\n## 10-a. What variables in the raw data is `output` *Running speed* derived from?\n\ni. Derived from `ft_RunSpeed`, the per-frame running speed.\n\nii.\n```python\nspeed_bin = digitize_speed(np.asarray(spec.record[\"ft_RunSpeed\"], dtype=np.float32)[frame_idx], speed_edges)\n```\n\niii. Running speed is a behavioral variable recorded alongside imaging frames.\n\n## 10-b. What processing is involved in computing `output` *Running speed*?\n\ni. Raw `ft_RunSpeed` values are read at retained frame indices. No smoothing or interpolation.\n\nii.\n```python\ndef digitize_speed(speed: np.ndarray, edges: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:\n    bins = np.searchsorted(edges, speed, side=\"right\")\n    return np.clip(bins, 0, 3).astype(np.int16)\n```\n\niii. The AI computes global quartile edges from all retained running frames across all sessions before applying binning.\n\n## 10-c. How is `output` *Running speed* thresholded into categories?\n\ni. Global quartile edges are computed at [25%, 50%, 75%] from all retained running frames across all retained sessions. Speeds are binned into 4 quartile categories (q1, q2, q3, q4) using `np.searchsorted`.\n\nii.\n```python\nspeed_values = np.concatenate(all_speeds).astype(np.float32, copy=False)\nspeed_edges = np.quantile(speed_values, [0.25, 0.5, 0.75]).astype(np.float32)\n```\n\niii. The resulting distribution is exactly [0.25, 0.25, 0.25, 0.25] by construction, matching the decoder task specification of \"4 bins, each corresponding to 25% of the data.\"\n\n## 10-d. How is `output` *Running speed* aligned with the neural data?\n\ni. Speed values are read at the same retained frame indices as neural data.\n\nii. (Same code as 10-a)\n\niii. Same `frame_idx` for all data streams.\n\n## 11. How are minor mistakes in the data, e.g. missing data, handled?\n\ni. Several edge cases are handled:\n- Behavior frame arrays are truncated to the neural frame count (`[:nfr]`), since behavior arrays are 1-3 frames longer\n- NaN values in `ft_trInd` are excluded via `np.isfinite` check\n- NaN values in `LickFr` and `LickTrind` are filtered out\n- Trials with fewer than 5 retained timepoints are excluded\n- Trials with retained duration > 60s are excluded (sparse stalled trials)\n- Trials with interframe gaps > 10s are excluded\n- Sessions with fewer than 2 valid trials are excluded\n- When no valid stimulus pair is found for neuron selection, all mapped neurons are kept as fallback\n\nii.\n```python\nft_tr = np.asarray(record[\"ft_trInd\"][:nfr], dtype=float)\nvalid = np.isfinite(ft_tr)\n...\nvalid = np.isfinite(lick_frames) & np.isfinite(lick_trial)\n...\nif stim_pair is None:\n    return np.arange(spk.shape[0], dtype=np.int32)\n```\n\niii. The AI documented that 2,217 trials were removed by the additional quality filters and that no sessions dropped below 2 valid trials. The behavior-neural frame mismatch is handled by the standard `[:nfr]` truncation matching the reference code.\n\n## 12-a. What are the most time-consuming steps of the code?\n\ni. The most time-consuming step is loading the full neural recording files (up to 89,577 neurons x ~29K frames per session). The neuron selection step (`select_decoder_neurons`) also requires loading the full spike matrix to compute d-prime, adding significant computation. The full conversion takes ~635 seconds for 89 sessions.\n\nii.\n```python\nspk = load_spike_matrix(root, spec.base)  # loads full neuron x frame matrix\n...\nselected_neurons = select_decoder_neurons(spk, spec.record, region_idx)  # computes d' on full matrix\n```\n\niii. The AI estimated ~10.4 seconds per session and noted that I/O is the primary bottleneck.\n\n## 12-b. What loops in the code could have been vectorized to improve efficiency?\n\ni. The per-trial loop in `process_sessions` iterates over each trial to construct neural, input, and output arrays. Some of this (e.g., computing time_to_cue and time_since_start for all frames at once, then splitting) could be vectorized. The per-area loop in `select_decoder_neurons` (4 iterations) is minor.\n\nii.\n```python\nfor trial_idx, frame_idx in enumerate(spec.trial_frame_indices):\n    ...\n    neural_trial = spk[:, frame_idx]\n    time_to_cue, time_since_start = session_trial_info(...)\n    ...\n```\n\niii. The AI focused optimization on minimizing I/O and doing retinotopy/lick processing once per session rather than per trial.\n\n## 12-c. What processing does the code repeat multiple times?\n\ni. Trial quality checks are performed twice: once in `prepare_session_specs` (to compute speed quartiles) and again in the main `process_sessions` loop. Running speed values are also collected twice for the same reason.
